Reading Paper 1: the forced line-element argument and Killing horizon (Kerr) extension

I've finished the first paper and identified the key insight: the Metric Singularity Theorem shows that when a null geodesic is both null-separated and spatially coincident, the proper time interval must vanish—this distinguishes it from generic null hypersurfaces. Applying this to Schwarzschild, events crossing the horizon maintain the same invariant areal radius along their generator, satisfying both conditions and confirming the metric singularity.

Now I'm seeing how this extends universally: along any generator, the spatial coordinates freeze while only time evolves, forcing the metric component to vanish and defining the Killing horizon condition. This applies to Kerr as well. The Asymptotic Alignment Lemma shows that exterior slices accumulate on the horizon with their tangent hyperplanes becoming asymptotically aligned, and the lapse diverges as we approach infinite exterior time—the horizon is fundamentally an infinite-time phenomenon from the outside perspective. There's also a distinction between horizons (finite curvature) and singularities at the origin (infinite curvature), two species within the same genus differing only in their curvature properties.

I'm tracking three core computations to re-derive across the papers: the horizon-locus eigenvalues from the cycloid critical points, the involution through root-exchange symmetry, and the vacuum-kernel ODE from the slicing operator formalism.

I'm looking at Daryl's core insight about the hole argument—the moment where Einstein's conceptual breakthrough becomes clear. This is the foundational thinking that ties together the entire problem he was grappling with, and it seems to be cut off mid-sentence as he's describing that pivotal realization. The layer itself is what individuates solutions — the 4D manifold is just how we represent it. When the hole argument reveals two metrics describing the same physics, it's really showing two different slicings of one underlying layer. The manifold points can't individuate because the manifold isn't what actually exists; the layer is. This gives the hole argument its missing positive answer: the ontological primitive that resolves the apparent indeterminism. Once you have the layer as the actual object, gauge freedom and multiple representations stop being paradoxes and become natural consequences of representing one thing in different ways — the same way the taxonomy dissolves confusion by supplying the point/place/existent distinction.

The amount of representational freedom (metric non-uniqueness) is graded by the symmetry preserved in a cut, which depends on how much matter bends the geometry. In vacuum, where there's no bending, the cut sits in the substrate's symmetric family and representational freedom is maximal; at the wall where inhomogeneity makes geometry rigid, this freedom vanishes. The key is keeping two things distinct: gauge diffeomorphism freedom (the representational covariance everywhere, the fiber the hole argument exploits) versus genuine isometry and isotropy (actual symmetries of the solution, strongest in vacuum, absent at the wall). They're not the same thing, but they converge at the hole — a matter-free region is an unbent cut where the substrate's symmetry is most visible and representational freedom most apparent.

Cartan geometry extends this further: the standard hole argument shows that diffeo-related metrics represent the same physics within a single geometry, but CR pushes beyond that to treat genuinely distinct Lorentzian geometries (like different de Sitter slicings) as different representations of one underlying substrate layer. The algebroid I'm constructing needs to hold both the gauge freedom within a geometry and the covariance between distinct geometries.

The hole argument, the problem of time, and the question of what individuates spacetime are really three perspectives on the same underlying issue—manifesting through chart degeneracy, diffeomorphism gauge freedom, and temporal foliations respectively.

The core insight is that each framework commits the same category error: treating the representation as the existent. Whether it's reifying the manifold point as a physical individual, the block as what's real, or the frozen constraint as fundamental, the error dissolves once we recognize the existent belongs to the smooth substrate, the layer, or the advancing 3-space. The programme's signature is dissolution rather than solution—Einstein grasped the negative half, and layer-ontology completes it as the positive half.

The problem of time in canonical form mirrors the hole argument's structure: the frozen constraint embodies gauge freedom across time, where every foliation is equivalent and no preferred time exists, just as resolving the hole argument requires giving an equivalence class through a clock section.

Now I'm recognizing that the algebroid plus layer ontology plus clock mechanism addresses all three problems at once. The cut-deformation maps onto the pair of Hamiltonian and momentum constraints, and the bracket that must close is the ADM constraint algebra—the Dirac hypersurface-deformation algebra. This is famously not a Lie algebra because it has structure functions rather than constants; the Hamiltonian-Hamiltonian bracket contains the metric itself. The key move is recognizing that the algebroid structure is precisely the right home for this constraint algebra, since hypersurface deformation has structure functions over the base, making it a Lie algebroid rather than a Lie algebra. So the problem of time dissolves once you see that the constraint algebra is fundamentally algebroid in nature, and this connects to the vision of the so(4,1)⋉𝒞 action algebroid and the ADM constraint.

Now I'm testing whether the cut-deformation bracket reproduces the hypersurface-deformation algebra with the substrate metric as the structure function—that's the falsifiable test. They're related but distinct, and while the action algebroid and the hypersurface-deformation algebroid resonate deeply, I shouldn't claim they're identical.

The key move is to call the action algebroid the HOME and the constraint algebra the DYNAMICAL FACE, treating their potential unification as an open question rather than asserting sameness. The core work ahead is building the algebroid as a unified object—connecting the momentum sector (diffeo constraint as shift-departure) with the bracket structure, identifying the cut-deformation bracket with the ADM hypersurface-deformation algebroid where structure functions define what makes it an algebroid, then imposing the dS₅-slicing restriction to determine which cuts are admissible via infinitesimal transformations.

I'm reviewing the key findings from the source material, particularly around the forced-versus-chosen clocks problem and how it maps across multiple layers grounded in the canonical paper's sections on mechanics.

At the mechanism level, the deparametrization is entirely standard—the minisuperspace clock is of the Brown–Kuchař type, and a relationalist runs the identical computation, so "forced" carries no formal distinction here. At late-time observables, both approaches reproduce flat ΛCDM identically; a relationalist choosing the cosmic-dust clock recovers everything the canonical approach gets by forcing it, so nothing measured distinguishes the two. But at the multiple-choice problem itself—the genuine pathology where different clocks yield inequivalent quantizations—relationalism has no principle to break the tie, leaving it as a standing open problem. The canonical approach resolves this by denying the premise: one clock is physically real, so one quantization is physical, while the others are merely quantizations of clocks that don't exist.

The non-circularity hinges on establishing cosmic time from cosmological evidence—the CMB, parallax analogs—that precedes any quantization question entirely. The arrow runs from evidence to clock to quantization, never backward; the canonical approach doesn't posit a preferred frame to solve the quantization problem, so it avoids assuming what it's trying to justify. This mirrors the hole argument's structure: just as Einstein resolved the underdetermination of bare manifold points by denying they're individuated, the multiple-choice problem arises from underdetermination in the bare formalism itself. The relationalist position holds that the CMB frame emerges from our specific matter distribution rather than from fundamental law, so the foliation remains gauge-equivalent at the constraint level—CR doesn't formally refute this, but instead rejects the underlying premise on grounds of occurrence and existence (the layer genuinely advances; the foliation is merely its representation). The real disagreement hinges on a reading grounded in occurrence, existence, and the CMB itself, not on formal logic alone, and CR's completion of the hole argument turns on this exact point.

CR addresses the multiple-choice problem at the level of warrant—the forced clock selects one quantization—rather than delivering a completed quantization; the physical Hamiltonian corresponds to the closed-S³ model, while operator-ordering and self-adjoint extensions remain open questions, with full SdS canonical quantization as the next target. The falsifiable edge lies in whether the forced clock generates a prediction that the relationalist can't reproduce through relabeling, and that prediction isn't in the canonical paper but in the cosmology work.

The discriminant emerges from early-universe expansion: if no radiation contributes to the expansion rate, then geometry alone sets the dynamics, which could shift the sound horizon and acoustic scale away from standard ΛCDM predictions. This isn't merely interpretive—it's empirical. The relationalist has no principled way to single out this congruence, so the cosmic frame becomes a genuine constraint rather than a notational choice. The hole argument, the multiple-choice problem, and the CMB discriminant converge on the same seam, and the foundational move is to treat the hole argument as its own paper and flip the script.

Daryl's point cuts through: the hole-argument note already exists in the cosmology paper, but it's buried as a minor application within a larger framework. The goal is to extract it, make it coherent on its own terms, and show how it unifies the corpus—not as an external reach, but as something foundational that reframes everything else. The corpus material is already there; it's about reorganizing and centering what's been written.

The unifying lens is the existent/representation distinction—a family of famous problems all trace back to the same error: treating a representation as if it were the thing itself, solved by identifying what individuates the object (the layer, the substrate). And this lens repeats recursively across different levels, like a nested ladder where the same structure appears at each rung.

Now I'm seeing how representational freedom in R manifests across multiple domains—coordinate values, derived quantities, whole geometries, manifold points, foliations—and how these cluster into two groups: the spatial cluster (which reifies geometric features but dissolves into slicings of one substrate with an invariant) and the foundational cluster (which reifies manifold and temporal structure but dissolves into the layer's existence and advancement). The rungs nest rather than stack, meeting at the substrate.
